**R12** (Part IV, Expectation, surprise, violation, lines 217-221)

Declaration: "Part IV now defines expectation and violation for every transport, and keeps surprise for a violation of a selected transport at a pair outside its history."

A claimed obligation (from Part XI) is a condition over stated occasions. A fidelity failure is when a transport's fidelity condition fails. These are different concepts.

So R13 says "a violation the system represents can be a recognized difficulty" — and Part X defines "recognized difficulty" in terms of obligations. The word "can be" is important — it says a represented violation CAN BE a recognized difficulty, not that it always is. So it's saying that in some cases, a represented violation qualifies as a recognized difficulty. This is a weaker claim than "every represented violation is a recognized difficulty."

So the claim is: it's possible for a represented violation to be a recognized difficulty. This would be the case when the violation constitutes a failure of a claimed obligation. The text doesn't say it always does. So this seems fine.

But does Part X's definition of "recognized difficulty" match? Part X says a recognized difficulty is "a failure of a claimed obligation, or a conflict in which what the system holds meets a claimed obligation only by failing a protected one (Part XI), when the system represents it." So it requires an obligation failure AND representation. R13 says a represented violation "can be" a recognized difficulty. **R21** (Part VI, Work, support, and interference, line 299)

Declaration: "Part VI now says that the supports assessed are the subsets of the written commitments, whether or not anyone has set them out, and not supports that need commitments outside them."

Old: "and the supports assessed are the ones actually written, not a support someone could write in their place."

New: "and the supports assessed are subsets of the written \(\Gamma\), whether or not anyone has set them out, not a support someone could write with commitments outside \(\Gamma\)."

Let me analyze:

Old says: "the supports assessed are the ones actually written, not a support someone could write in their place."

This has two claims:
1. The supports assessed are the ones actually written.
2. Not a support someone could write in their place.

"Actually written" suggests only supports that have been explicitly set out (written down). "A support someone could write in their place" suggests hypothetical alternative supports.

New says: "the supports assessed are subsets of the written \(\Gamma\), whether or not anyone has set them out, not a support someone could write with commitments outside \(\Gamma\)."

This has two claims:
1. The supports assessed are subsets of the written Γ, whether or not anyone has set them out.
2. Not a support someone could write with commitments outside Γ.

Key change: the old says "actually written" (only those that have been set out), while the new says "subsets of the written Γ, whether or not anyone has set them out" (all subsets of Γ, even if not explicitly written). This is a CLAIM change — it expands what counts as "assessed."

**R24** (Part VI, Hard-to-vary, line 313)

Declaration: "Part VI now says that (E) has no condition that each commitment do work; that a commitment such that every support stays a support when it is added and when it is removed does no work by itself in the candidate, leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged and is critical in no support; that when the commitments are infinitely many a block of such commitments can still be critical; and that how hard an account is to vary is a separate matter, shown by Pres, which grades nothing."

When \(\Gamma\) is infinite, a block of such commitments can still be critical (Infinitary support). How hard an account is to vary is a separate matter, shown by \(\operatorname{Pres}\), and it grades nothing."

The new wording correctly says "\(\{d\}\) is critical in no support" — the singleton block {d}. The declaration drops the curly braces and says "is critical in no support" — which is slightly imprecise (it should say "is critical in no support as a singleton" or "\(\{d\}\) is critical in no support").

But this is a minor imprecision in the declaration. The declaration is meant to say "exactly what changes in what is claimed." Hmm, actually, the declaration says "is critical in no support" — this is ambiguous. But I think in context, "is critical in no support" means "is not critical (as a block) in any support" — i.e., the block consisting of just d is not critical. This matches the text. ✓

Now, let me check for coherence issues.

"A commitment \(d\) does no work by itself in the candidate when every support stays a support after \(d\) is added to it and after \(d\) is removed from it" — Let me parse this:

- "every support stays a support after d is added to it": for every W ∈ S, W ∪ {d} ∈ S.
- "every support stays a support after d is removed from it": for every W ∈ S, W \ {d} ∈ S.

Wait, "after d is added to it" — "it" = the support. So for every support W, adding d to W keeps it a support. And "after d is removed from it" — removing d from W keeps it a support.

But what if d ∉ W? Then "removing d from W" = W (no change). And "adding d to W" = W ∪ {d}. So the conditions are:
1. For every W ∈ S: W ∪ {d} ∈ S. (Adding d never destroys support.)
2. For every W ∈ S: W \ {d} ∈ S. (Removing d never destroys support.)

Condition 2 is trivially true when d ∉ W (W \ {d} = W). When d ∈ W, it says W \ {d} ∈ S (removing d keeps it a support).

So the combined condition says: d is neither necessary nor sufficient for any support. Adding it doesn't help; removing it doesn't hurt.

"does no work by itself" — this seems right. A commitment that neither helps nor hurts is doing no work.

"a candidate carrying \(d\) then meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without \(d\)" — if we remove d from Γ (and from the candidate), the candidate's standing under (E) is unchanged. Since d is neither necessary nor sufficient, removing it doesn't change whether Account holds.

But wait — Account depends on the four conditions (F1), (F2), (A), NonCircular, NonVacuous. These depend on the commitments Γ and their behavior. If d is removed from Γ, does Account change?

The conditions involve:
- (F1): component fidelity — depends on all active components (which are the commitments in Γ).
- (F2): assembled agreement — depends on the whole organization E.
- (A): question fidelity — depends on the answer profile.
- NonCircular: depends on the active commitments and their effect on the answer.
- NonVacuity: depends on the contract and solutions.

If d is removed from Γ (but remains as a component of E outside Γ), then:
- (F1) is checked for "every active component k of E" — active components are those in Γ. So removing d from Γ means d is no longer active, and (F1) is not checked for d. This could change (F1): if d was the only component that failed (F1), removing it from Γ would make (F1) hold.

Hmm wait, but the condition says "every support stays a support after d is added to it and after d is removed from it." A "support" is a W ∈ S, where S = {W ⊆ Γ : Account(E|W, p)}. So a support is a subset of Γ that satisfies Account. If d is removed from Γ entirely, the new Γ' = Γ \ {d}. The supports of the new candidate are S' = {W ⊆ Γ' : Account(E|W, p)}.

The claim is: "a candidate carrying d then meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d." This means: Account(E with Γ) holds iff Account(E with Γ\{d}) holds. In other words, d's presence in Γ doesn't affect whether the full candidate satisfies Account.

But the condition is about supports (subsets of Γ), not about the full Γ. If every support stays a support when d is added and when d is removed, does this imply that Γ ∈ S iff Γ\{d} ∈ S?

Let's see: the condition says for every W ∈ S: W ∪ {d} ∈ S and W \ {d} ∈ S. Take W = Γ (if Γ ∈ S). Then Γ \ {d} ∈ S (by condition 2). And (Γ \ {d}) ∪ {d} = Γ ∈ S (by condition 1 applied to Γ\{d}). So if Γ ∈ S, then Γ\{d} ∈ S.

What if Γ ∉ S? The conditions are vacuously about supports (members of S). If Γ ∉ S, the conditions don't directly say anything about Γ. But the claim is "a candidate carrying d meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d." If Γ ∉ S (the full candidate fails), then Γ\{d} might or might not be in S. The condition says every support stays a support when d is removed. If Γ\{d} ∈ S, then (Γ\{d}) ∪ {d} = Γ ∈ S (by condition 1), contradicting Γ ∉ S. So if Γ ∉ S and the conditions hold, then Γ\{d} ∉ S too. Hence the claim holds.

Wait, let me be more careful. The condition is: for every W ∈ S: W ∪ {d} ∈ S and W \ {d} ∈ S.

If Γ ∉ S: we need to show Γ\{d} ∉ S. Suppose Γ\{d} ∈ S. Then (Γ\{d}) ∪ {d} = Γ ∈ S (by condition 1). Contradiction. So Γ\{d} ∉ S. ✓

If Γ ∈ S: we need to show Γ\{d} ∈ S. Γ\{d} = Γ \ {d} ∈ S (by condition 2 applied to W = Γ). ✓

So the claim "a candidate carrying d meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d" follows from the conditions. ✓

Now, "\(\{d\}\) is critical in no support (B)" — criticality is defined as: CriticalBlock(B; W, p) iff W ∈ S ∧ W \ B ∉ S. For B = {d}: CriticalBlock({d}; W, p) iff W ∈ S ∧ W \ {d} ∉ S. The condition says for every W ∈ S: W \ {d} ∈ S. So W \ {d} ∉ S never holds when W ∈ S. Hence {d} is critical in no support. ✓

And "When Γ is infinite, a block of such commitments can still be critical (Infinitary support)." The infinitary support example: Γ = {d_n : |x| ≤ 1/n}. Every unbounded index set determines x = 0; no minimal support exists. Here, each d_n individually does no work (removing one doesn't affect the others' collective determination of x = 0), but the block of all d_n is critical (removing all of them loses the determination). But wait — the claim is about "a block of such commitments" (commitments that do no work by themselves). In the infinitary example, each d_n does no work by itself (since any unbounded subset determines x = 0, removing one d_n from an unbounded set still leaves it unbounded). But the block of ALL d_n is critical in the support W = Γ (since Γ \ Γ = ∅ doesn't determine x = 0).

Hmm wait, does each d_n satisfy the condition "every support stays a support after d is added to it and after d is removed from it"? Let me check. S = {W ⊆ Γ : W determines x = 0} = {W ⊆ Γ : W is unbounded}.For d_n: does every support stay a support when d_n is added? If W is unbounded, W ∪ {d_n} is still unbounded. ✓
Does every support stay a support when d_n is removed? If W is unbounded and d_n ∈ W, W \ {d_n} is still unbounded (since removing one element from an unbounded set keeps it unbounded). If d_n ∉ W, W \ {d_n} = W is unbounded. ✓

So each d_n satisfies the condition and "does no work by itself." But the block Γ = all d_n is critical in the support W = Γ (since Γ ∈ S but Γ \ Γ = ∅ ∉ S). ✓

So the claim "When Γ is infinite, a block of such commitments can still be critical" is correct. ✓

But wait — the definition of CriticalBlock says "For nonempty \(B\subseteq W\), CriticalBlock(B;W,p) iff W∈S_{E,p} ∧ W∖B ∉ S_{E,p}." For B = Γ and W = Γ: CriticalBlock(Γ; Γ, p) iff Γ ∈ S ∧ Γ \ Γ = ∅ ∉ S. Since Γ is unbounded (determines x = 0), Γ ∈ S. And ∅ doesn't determine x = 0, so ∅ ∉ S. Hence CriticalBlock(Γ; Γ, p) holds. ✓

So the block Γ (consisting entirely of commitments that do no work by themselves) is critical. ✓

Now, "How hard an account is to vary is a separate matter, shown by Pres, and it grades nothing." This is consistent with the earlier text: "How hard an account is to vary is a separate matter, shown by \(\operatorname{Pres}\), and it grades nothing." Wait, this appears in R24's new text. But is it also in R06's new text? Let me check...

(F1): "For every active component k of E..." — checks fidelity for each active component. This doesn't require each to "do work" (have a unique contribution); it just checks that each has the right relation.

(F2): "the assembled organization agrees" — this is about the whole, not individual commitments.

(A): "Ans_E(τ(a),σ(b)) = Ans_p(a,b)" — this is about the answer profile matching.

NonCircular: requires a nonempty block G ⊆ Γ such that the answer changes when G is deleted. This requires SOME commitments to do work (the block G), but not EACH one.

NonVacuity: about the contract and solutions.

So indeed, (E) has no condition that EACH commitment do work. ✓ (NonCircular only requires SOME block to do work.)

But wait — the infinitary support example uses constraints |x| ≤ 1/n. These are not exactly "supports" in the Part VI sense. Let me re-read...

"\(\Gamma=\{d_n:|x|\le 1/n\}\): every unbounded index set determines \(x=0\); no minimal support and no singleton witness exists."

Here, Γ is a set of constraints d_n, where d_n is the constraint |x| ≤ 1/n. A "support" W ⊆ Γ is a subset of these constraints. W "determines x = 0" when the constraints in W collectively force x = 0. An unbounded index set (a set of indices {n_i} with no upper bound) determines x = 0 (because the intersection of |x| ≤ 1/n_i for unbounded n_i is x = 0). A bounded index set doesn't (the intersection is |x| ≤ 1/N for some finite N, which allows x ≠ 0).

So S = {W ⊆ Γ : W's index set is unbounded}. This is consistent with the Part VI framework.

Each d_n does no work by itself (as shown above). The block of all d_n is critical in W = Γ (since Γ \ Γ = ∅, whose index set is bounded, so ∅ ∉ S). ✓

But the text says "(B) records the collective contribution." (B) is the CriticalBlock definition. CriticalBlock(Γ; Γ, p) holds (as shown). So (B) records the collective contribution of all d_n. ✓

OK, R24 seems to stand. But let me think about whether there's a coherence issue with the definition of "does no work by itself."

The definition says: "A commitment d does no work by itself in the candidate when every support stays a support after d is added to it and after d is removed from it."

This defines "does no work" as a property of d in a specific candidate. The condition involves "every support" — every W ∈ S_{E,p}. But S_{E,p} depends on Γ (the set of commitments). If d ∈ Γ, then S includes subsets of Γ. If we "add d to" a support W, we get W ∪ {d}. But W is already a subset of Γ, and d ∈ Γ. So W ∪ {d} ⊆ Γ. This is fine.

If d ∉ Γ, then "adding d to W" gives W ∪ {d}⊄ Γ (since d ∉ Γ). But S is defined as subsets of Γ. So W ∪ {d} ∉ S would be automatic (it's not a subset of Γ). This means "every support stays a support after d is added" would fail for any d ∉ Γ.

But the text says "A commitment d does no work by itself in the candidate" — d is a commitment in the candidate (d ∈ Γ). So d ∈ Γ and W ∪ {d} ⊆ Γ. ✓

Wait, but what about the case where d is NOT in Γ? The text says "a candidate carrying d" — so d ∈ Γ. And "(E) has no condition that each commitment do work" — referring to commitments in Γ. So d ∈ Γ is implicit. ✓

But "after d is removed from it" — removing d from W (a support). If d ∈ W, then W \ {d} ⊂ W. If d ∉ W, then W \ {d} = W. Both are subsets of Γ. ✓

OK, I think R24 is fine.

**R30** (Part X, Construction, lines 399-403)

Declaration: "Part X now says that an inexplicit representation is not an absent one; that (R), Deploy and Build ask for no particular format, only whether the relevant distinctions and transformations are instantiated, with the fidelity and provenance (R) requires, and used where use is asked; that a representation can be partial, distributed or extended in time and need not be explicit all at once; that retaining a critical target can consist in being able to re-present the relevant distinction; that a construction witness may identify a constructed binding by its use, in the manner of reason use; that use does not by itself construct, so received content used as received keeps its inherited provenance; and that a carrier that passes content on without such use has relayed it."

This is a large addition. Let me break it down and check each part.

New text (beyond the old wording "A small binding newly prepared inside received content is construction of that binding, and the rest of the content keeps its inherited provenance."):

Paragraph 1:
"An inexplicit representation is not an absent one. A pianist can imagine a passage without playing it. A geometer can manipulate a spatial relation without naming every component. An investigator can notice an inconsistency before articulating its premises. None of (R), Deploy and Build asks whether the relevant distinctions and transformations are written in a particular format: what they ask is whether those distinctions and transformations are instantiated, with the fidelity and provenance (R) requires, and used where use is asked."

Paragraph 2:
"A system's realization can use a partial, distributed, or temporally extended representation. The relevant bindings can be available through memory, imagery, action rehearsal, or interaction with an artifact. Not every representation must be simultaneously explicit. In the context of identifying a binding by its use: the binding encodes a mapping from content changes to response changes. When the system encounters a content change, the binding dictates what response change should follow. So "the changes the binding specifies" = "the response changes that the binding dictates for given content changes."

In the binding-identification context, the binding itself specifies the changes (since we're identifying what the binding does). In reason use, the deliberative rule specifies the changes (since we're evaluating whether the response uses the objection correctly according to the rule).

"a conflict in which what the system holds meets a claimed obligation only by failing a protected one" — this means: the system's current state satisfies a claimed obligation (o ∈ O) but only by violating a protected obligation (r ∈ P). What about N21 (the order of adjectives)? R30's situations include N21.

N21: "Ngozi is a native speaker of English. She says 'a lovely little old green French knife' and would never say 'a green French little old lovely knife'. Fixed verdict: "Yes, she knows it, tacitly. She formed her grasp of it herself."

But R30 also says "Use does not by itself construct: received content used as it was received keeps its inherited provenance." For N21: Ngozi heard examples of speech (received content) and formed the rule (construction). Using the examples (hearing speech) doesn't construct the rule — she constructed it herself. So the rule is her own construction (the fixed verdict says "She formed her grasp of it herself"). ✓

And "The examples came from other people, and the rule itself was already her community's convention, which she rebuilt rather than invented." (fixed verdict) — the rule is the community's convention (inherited content), but Ngozi's grasp of it is her own construction (she rebuilt it). This is consistent with R30: "A small binding newly prepared inside received content is construction of that binding, and the rest of the content keeps its inherited provenance." But there might be an issue with R30's criteria for identifying a binding by its use. For N21: Ngozi's binding (grasp of the rule) is identified by her use (getting adjective order right in new sentences). The criteria are: "responses that preserve its role bindings, send content-preserving recodings to the same transition and content changes to the changes the binding specifies, and lie on an active route."

For Ngozi: her responses (adjective order in new sentences) preserve role bindings (the order constraints). Content-preserving recodings (different words, same structure) go to the same transition (same ordering). Content changes (different adjectives) go to the changes the binding specifies (the ordering rule). And they lie on an active route (her speech production).

But the criterion "content changes to the changes the binding specifies" — for Ngozi, the binding (rule) specifies how adjective properties map to ordering. "The changes the binding specifies" = the ordering changes that the rule determines. When the content changes (different adjectives), the response changes (different ordering) according to the rule. ✓

This is consistent. But as I noted earlier, the criterion differs from Part IX's "the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule." For Ngozi, there is no explicit "deliberative rule" — the rule is tacit. The "binding" (her grasp of the rule) IS the operative rule. So "the changes the binding specifies" = "the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule" (since the binding IS the rule). In this case, the two formulations agree.

But in general (for other bindings that aren't deliberative rules), they might differ. The coherence issue is about the general claim, not about specific cases.

OK, let me now think about the R30 coherence issue more carefully. Is it really a defect?

The new text says: "A construction witness may therefore identify a binding constructed in the subhistory by its use rather than by a statement of it: by responses that preserve its role bindings, send content-preserving recodings to the same transition and content changes to the changes the binding specifies, and lie on an active route, as reason use asks of an objection (Part IX)."

The phrase "as reason use asks of an objection (Part IX)" — "as reason use asks" is a comparison. It says "in the way that reason use asks." But the criteria listed are not identical to reason use's criteria. Specifically:
- Reason use: "sends content changes to the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule"
- New text: "content changes to the changes the binding specifies"

For a binding that is a deliberative rule, these agree. For a binding that is not a deliberative rule (e.g., a structural connection like "persistence component to continuity subnetwork"), "the changes the binding specifies" is about structural changes, while "the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule" is about deliberative changes.

But in the context of "identifying a binding by its use," the binding's use is characterized by how it responds to changes. The "changes the binding specifies" are the changes the binding determines (its response pattern). This is analogous to reason use's "changes specified by the operative deliberative rule" (the response pattern determined by the rule).

So the analogy is: just as a response uses an objection when it follows the deliberative rule's specified changes, a binding is identified by its use when responses follow the binding's specified changes. The "operative deliberative rule" in reason use plays the same role as "the binding" in binding identification.

But the text says "as reason use asks of an objection (Part IX)" — suggesting the criteria are the same. They're not exactly the same (one uses "the operative deliberative rule," the other uses "the binding"). But the role is analogous.

Is this a coherence issue? I think it is — the pointer to Part IX suggests the criteria match, but one criterion is differently worded. A reader might be confused about whether the criterion is about the binding or about the deliberative rule.

But is it a SERIOUS issue? The criterion is stated in full ("content changes to the changes the binding specifies"), so a reader can use it. The pointer to Part IX is for comparison. The comparison is imperfect but not wrong — the criteria are analogous.

I think this is a minor coherence issue. Let me note it but not make it the centerpiece.

Actually wait, I want to double-check R24 one more time. The declaration says "a commitment such that every support stays a support when it is added and when it is removed does no work by itself in the candidate, leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged and is critical in no support."

Let me parse: "a commitment such that [condition] does no work by itself in the candidate, leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged and is critical in no support."

So the commitment (satisfying the condition) has three properties:
1. Does no work by itself.
2. Leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged.
3. Is critical in no support.

The new wording says: "A commitment d does no work by itself in the candidate when every support stays a support after d is added to it and after d is removed from it: a candidate carrying d then meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d, and {d} is critical in no support (B)."

So:
1. "does no work by itself" — defined as "every support stays a support after d is added and after d is removed."
2. "a candidate carrying d then meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d" — this is a consequence ("then"), not a definition.
3. "{d} is critical in no support (B)" — this is also a consequence.

The declaration says the commitment "does no work by itself... leaves the candidate's standing... unchanged and is critical in no support." But the new wording says "does no work by itself WHEN [condition]: [consequences]." So "does no work by itself" is defined by the condition, and the consequences follow.

The declaration says all three (does no work, leaves standing unchanged, is critical in no) as properties of the commitment. The new wording says "does no work" is defined by the condition, and the other two follow. This is slightly different — the declaration treats all three as definitional, while the text treats only the first as definitional and the others as consequences.

But the declaration says "does no work by itself in the candidate, leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged and is critical in no support" — the comma after "candidate" separates "does no work" from "leaves... unchanged." The sentence structure is ambiguous: it could mean "the commitment does no work, leaves standing unchanged, and is critical in no" (three parallel properties) or "the commitment that does no work also leaves standing unchanged and is critical in no" (first is definitional, others are consequences).

The declaration is ambiguous. But the new wording is clear: "does no work by itself... WHEN [condition]: [consequences]." So the condition DEFINES "does no work" and the consequences follow.

I think the declaration is slightly imprecise but not wrong. The three properties are all stated in the new wording. The declaration lists them. The relationship between them (definitional vs. consequential) is not captured, but all three are present. ✓

Actually wait, the declaration says "leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged." But the new wording says "a candidate carrying d then meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d." "Leaves standing unchanged" means removing d doesn't change whether (E) holds. "Meets (E) exactly when it meets (E) without d" means (E) holds with d iff (E) holds without d. These are equivalent. ✓

But "leaves the candidate's standing under (E) unchanged" could also mean "d doesn't affect (E) at all" — not just that (E) holds iff it holds without d, but that d has no effect on the evaluation of (E). The new wording says "(E) holds iff it holds without d" — which is about the truth value of (E), not about the evaluation process. These are slightly different.

But in context, "standing under (E)" means "whether (E) holds." So "leaves standing unchanged" means "doesn't change whether (E) holds." This is equivalent to "(E) holds iff it holds without d." ✓

Now, one more check for R06. The declaration says "that a false theory offered as an answer is an explanatory candidate which ordinary usage may still call an explanation." But the new wording says "a false theory offered as an answer is an explanatory candidate, which ordinary usage may still call an explanation." The declaration drops the comma and says "an explanatory candidate which ordinary usage may still call an explanation." This could be read as "an explanatory candidate that ordinary usage may still call an explanation" (restrictive clause) or "an explanatory candidate, which ordinary usage may still call an explanation" (non-restrictive clause). The new wording uses a comma (non-restrictive), meaning ALL false theories offered as answers are explanatory candidates, and (separately) ordinary usage may call them explanations. The declaration without the comma might be read restrictively (only those that ordinary usage calls explanations are candidates). But in context, the declaration is summarizing the claim, and the comma difference is minor.

Let me also double-check R07 one more time.

The declaration says: "Part I's substrate independence now holds only so far as the adopted physics lets contents pass between physical media; where two media cannot exchange what they bear, the contents only one of them can bear form a barrier, in Part XIII's sense, for a system built of the other."

The new wording has three claims:
1. "Which organizations a carrier can bear, and whether what carriers of one physical medium bear can pass to carriers of another, are fixed by the adopted physics" — NOT in the declaration.
2. "substrate independence holds so far as that physics lets contents pass between media" — IN the declaration. ✓
3. "Where two media cannot exchange what they bear, the contents that only one of them can bear form, for a system built of the other, a barrier in the sense of Part XIII." — IN the declaration. ✓

So the declaration misses claim #1. This is a real issue. The declaration is incomplete.

But is claim #1 a new claim? The existing text says "Every attribution of an organization to a physical system must be permitted by the adopted physics." This constrains attributions. Claim #1 says "Which organizations a carrier can bear... are fixed by the adopted physics." This is about what determines what can be borne. "Fixed by" is stronger than "permitted by" — "fixed" implies determinacy (physics determines the full set), while "permitted" only implies a constraint (you can't go beyond physics, but physics might not determine the full set).

So claim #1 IS a new claim (stronger than the existing constraint). And the declaration misses it. This makes the declaration incomplete. Task (a) asks "Does the new wording claim more than the declaration says?" — Yes, claim #1 is more than the declaration says.

This is a defect in R07. The declaration is incomplete.

But is this a serious defect? The missing claim is about the determinacy of what carriers can bear. This is an important metaphysical claim (physics determines the full set of bearable organizations). The declaration should capture it.

Let me also think about the barrier claim's coherence with Part XIII.

Part XIII: "An explanatory barrier is an independently characterized domain for which every admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition leaves the relevant capability unavailable."

R07: "the contents that only one of them can bear form, for a system built of the other, a barrier in the sense of Part XIII."

For this to be a barrier in Part XIII's sense:
- "Independently characterized domain" — the contents that only one medium can bear are characterized by the physics. ✓
- "Every admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition leaves the relevant capability unavailable" — for a system built of medium A, no enabling condition can make it bear contents that require medium B (if the physics prevents it). ✓ (assuming the system is strictly built of medium A)

But "the relevant capability" — what capability? Part XIII's barriers are "explanatory barriers" — about the capability to explain. R07's barrier is about the capability to bear/represent certain contents. Are these the same?

If you can't represent a content, you can't explain it. So a representational barrier implies an explanatory barrier. But the converse isn't necessarily true (you might be able to represent something but not explain it). So R07's barrier (representational) is a SUBSET of Part XIII's barriers (explanatory). The claim is that these contents form a barrier "in the sense of Part XIII" — meaning they're a specific kind of Part XIII barrier. This seems right. ✓

But there's a subtlety: Part XIII says "A bypass refutes a proposed barrier." Could there be a bypass? If the system can use a translator or interface to the other medium, it might be able to access the contents. But the claim is about "a system built of the other" — a system that only uses one medium. If you add a translator, you've changed the system.

But "enabling conditions" in Part XIII might include external aids. "\(\operatorname{Enable}(s,T,\chi)\) holds when \(\chi\) is an admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition for \(s\) and the task \(T\)." An enabling condition could be "give the system a translator." If this is admitted and non-question-begging, it would be a bypass.

But the claim is about "a system built of the other" — constraining the system's construction. If the system is strictly built of one medium (no access to the other), then no enabling condition based on that medium's construction can help. But an enabling condition could be "rebuild the system with the other medium" — which changes the system.

Part XIII's barriers are about a specific system s: "for which every admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition leaves the relevant capability unavailable." The enabling conditions are for THIS system s. If s is built of medium A, then enabling conditions for s are things you can do to/with s. If s can't access medium B, then no enabling condition for s can give it medium B's contents.

But what if an enabling condition is "give s a medium-B interface"? This is admitted (physically possible) and non-question-begging. But it changes s (by adding an interface). Is this an enabling condition for s, or does it create a new system?

Part XIII says "Enable(s,T,χ) holds when χ is an admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition for s and the task T." If χ is "add a medium-B interface to s," this is an enabling condition for s (it's something done to s to help with task T). If it's admitted and non-question-begging, it would make the capability available — refuting the barrier.

So the barrier claim might not hold if enabling conditions can modify the system. But the claim is about "a system built of the other" — a system whose construction uses only one medium. Adding an interface might violate this constraint.

Hmm, this is getting complicated. The key question is: what counts as an "enabling condition" in Part XIII? If it can include modifying the system (adding interfaces), then the barrier might not hold. If it's restricted to conditions that don't change the system's construction, then the barrier holds.

Part XIII doesn't specify what counts as an "admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition." This is left open. So the barrier claim's match with Part XIII is imperfect — it depends on what counts as an enabling condition.

I think this is a minor coherence issue. The declaration says "a barrier, in Part XIII's sense" — and the match is imperfect but reasonable. The claim would be more precise if it specified what counts as an enabling condition.

OK, let me also reconsider R07. The declaration misses claim #1 ("which organizations a carrier can bear... are fixed by the adopted physics"). But is claim #1 really new? "Every attribution... must be permitted by the adopted physics" — physics constrains attributions.

Claim #1: "Which organizations a carrier can bear... are fixed by the adopted physics."

"Any carrier may bear an organization" + "attributions must be permitted by physics" ≈ "what a carrier can bear is constrained by physics."

But claim #1 says "fixed by the adopted physics" — which is stronger. "Fixed" means physics determines the complete set of bearable organizations. "Constrained" means physics puts limits on it. "Fixed" = "constrained from above AND below" (physics determines both what can and can't be borne). "Constrained" = "limited from above" (physics puts a ceiling on what can be borne).

In context, "Any carrier may bear an organization" suggests broad possibility (any carrier can bear something). "Every attribution must be permitted by physics" suggests constraint (can't attribute what physics forbids). Together: any carrier can bear some organization, but what it can bear is constrained by physics.

Claim #1 says what a carrier can bear is "fixed" by physics — determined completely. This is stronger than "constrained." It says physics determines not just what CAN'T be borne (constraint) but also what CAN be (determinacy).

So claim #1 IS a new claim (stronger than the existing text). And the declaration misses it. This is a defect.

But is it a serious defect? The missing claim is about the determinacy of bearable organizations. This is a metaphysical claim about the relationship between physics and representation. It's important but the declaration misses it.

Hmm, but "fixed" and "permitted" are different. "Permitted" means the physics allows it (doesn't forbid it). "Fixed" means the physics determines it (completely specifies what's possible). In a complete physical theory, these might be equivalent (if the physics is complete, what it permits is what it fixes). But in an incomplete theory, they differ.

Since the document says "the adopted physics" (suggesting a specific physical theory), and the physical module is defined as the sole source of physical facts, "fixed by the adopted physics" might mean "determined by the physical module." And "permitted by the adopted physics" might mean "allowed by the physical module." In a complete module, these are equivalent.

But the document doesn't say the physical module is complete. So "fixed" and "permitted" might differ.

Hmm, actually let me reconsider R12 one more time. I want to make sure the expectation definition is really problematic.

"the **expectation** is \(\operatorname{Ans}_S(\tau(a),\sigma(b))\)"

Let me think about what τ(a) is. t = (π,τ,σ,λ) is a transport from D to E. τ translates edits: τ: A_D → A_E (or something like that). So τ(a) is an edit of E.

Ans_S is the answer profile of S (the simulation layer). Ans_S takes an edit-boundary pair and returns a value in Y_S. So Ans_S(τ(a),σ(b)) requires τ(a) to be an edit that S can process.

If E = S (the transport is from D to S), then τ(a) is an edit of S, and Ans_S(τ(a),σ(b)) is well-defined. ✓

If E ≠ S, then τ(a) is an edit of E, not S. Ans_S(τ(a),σ(b)) requires τ(a) to be interpretable by S. Unless there's a mapping from E's edits to S's edits, this is undefined. ✗

So for a general transport from D to E (where E ≠ S), the expectation Ans_S(τ(a),σ(b)) is undefined. This is a coherence issue.

But in the two-layer setup, the key transport is from P to S. For this transport, E = S, and the expectation is well-defined. The preamble "Let t be a transport" in the revised text could mean "a transport in the two-layer setup" (from P to S). In that case, the expectation is well-defined.

Let me also reconsider one more time the barrier claim in R07. The declaration says "a barrier, in Part XIII's sense." Let me check the match more carefully.

Part XIII: "An explanatory barrier is an independently characterized domain for which every admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition leaves the relevant capability unavailable."

R07: "the contents that only one of them can bear form, for a system built of the other, a barrier in the sense of Part XIII."

For this to be a barrier:
1. "Independently characterized domain" — the contents are characterized by the physics (which determines what each medium can bear). ✓
2. "Every admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition leaves the relevant capability unavailable" — for a system built of medium A, no enabling condition can give it medium B's contents.

But point 2 requires that EVERY enabling condition fails. What counts as an enabling condition? Part XIII says "\(\operatorname{Enable}(s,T,\chi)\) holds when \(\chi\) is an admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition for \(s\) and the task \(T\)."

If χ = "give the system a medium-B translator" — this is an admitted (physically possible) and non-question-begging enabling condition. But it makes the capability available (the system can now access medium B's contents through the translator). So this χ is a bypass.

But the claim is about "a system built of the other" — a system constructed entirely from one medium. Adding a translator changes the system (it now has access to the other medium). Is this still "a system built of the other"?

If "built of the other" means "constructed entirely from one medium, with no access to the other," then adding a translator violates this constraint. The system is no longer "built of the other" alone. So the bypass doesn't apply to the original system.

But "enabling conditions" in Part XIII might not be restricted to non-modifying conditions. If χ can modify the system (add a translator), then it's a bypass. If χ is restricted to non-modifying conditions (training, etc.), then it might not be a bypass.

Part XIII doesn't specify what counts as an "admitted, non-question-begging enabling condition." This is left open. So the barrier claim's match with Part XIII depends on this specification.

I think the match is imperfect but reasonable. The declaration says "a barrier, in Part XIII's sense" — and the match is approximate. This is a minor coherence issue.

Actually, let me reconsider. Part XIII says "A finite list of failures is not a barrier proof; a bypass refutes a proposed barrier." So a barrier must withstand all bypasses. If adding a translator is a bypass, the barrier claim fails.

But the claim is about "a system built of the other" — constraining the system's construction. If the system's boundary is fixed (built of one medium only), then adding a translator is not a valid bypass (it changes the system). So the barrier holds within the constrained system.

But Part XIII's barriers are about a specific system s. If s is "built of medium A," then enabling conditions for s are things you can do to/with s without changing its construction. Adding a translator changes the construction. So it's not a valid enabling condition for s.

But what if an enabling condition is "rebuild s with medium B"? This changes the system (new construction). Is this a valid enabling condition? If s is defined by its construction (built of medium A), then rebuilding with medium B creates a new system s'. The enabling condition is for s', not s.

I think the barrier claim is reasonable: for a system built of one medium, no enabling condition for THAT system can give it the other medium's contents. This matches Part XIII's barrier definition (with the system's boundary fixed). ✓

But the declaration says "a barrier, in Part XIII's sense" — and the match is approximate (depends on what counts as an enabling condition). This is a minor issue.

OK, I think the main issue with R07 is the declaration incompleteness (missing claim #1). The barrier match is a secondary issue.

Actually, one more reconsideration. For R30, is the difference between "the changes the binding specifies" and "the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule" really a defect? Let me think about this from the perspective of what the text is trying to do.

R30 is about identifying a binding by its use. The binding is a structural connection (e.g., persistence component to continuity subnetwork). Its "use" is how the system behaves when the binding is active. The criteria for identifying this use are modeled on reason use.

In reason use, the response is characterized by:
1. Preserving role bindings.
2. Sending content-preserving recodings to the same transition.
3. Sending content changes to the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule.
4. Landing on an active route.

In R30, the binding's use is characterized by:
1. Preserving role bindings.
2. Sending content-preserving recodings to the same transition.
3. Content changes to the changes the binding specifies.
4. Lying on an active route.

Criterion 3 in R30 replaces "the operative deliberative rule" with "the binding." For a binding that is a structural connection, "the changes the binding specifies" = the structural changes the connection determines. For reason use's "the operative deliberative rule," "the changes specified" = the deliberative changes the rule determines.

These are different in general. But for the purpose of identifying a binding by its use, "the changes the binding specifies" is the right criterion (we want to see if the binding's structural behavior is preserved). Reason use's criterion is about deliberative behavior.

So R30 is intentionally adapting reason use's criteria for a different purpose (binding identification vs. objection use). The adaptation replaces "the operative deliberative rule" with "the binding" because the binding is what's being identified.

But the text says "as reason use asks of an objection (Part IX)" — suggesting the criteria are from reason use unchanged. The adaptation is not acknowledged.

I think the defect is: the text says the criteria are "as reason use asks" but adapts one criterion without acknowledging the adaptation. The pointer to Part IX is misleading because Part IX's definition differs.

Is this enough for R30 to fall? The criteria are self-contained and correct for the purpose. But the pointer is misleading and the declaration is imprecise. I think this is a defect but not a fatal one.

The text says "as reason use asks" but the criteria differ from reason use. A reader going to Part IX would find different criteria. This is confusing but not contradictory.

## Report

### 1. R12 — (a) and (c): the declaration claims expectation is defined for every transport, but the definition uses Ans_S, which is the simulation layer's answer profile and is not defined for a general transport.

The new preamble (lines 217-218) says "Let \(t\) be a transport with contract \(C\)" — using the general definition of transport from Part IV ("A transport from an organization \(D\) to an organization \(E\)"). The declaration claims "Part IV now defines expectation and violation for every transport." But the expectation (line 219) is "\(\operatorname{Ans}_S(\tau(a),\sigma(b))\)" — the answer profile of the simulation layer \(S\). In a transport \(t = (\pi, \tau, \sigma, \lambda)\) from \(D\) to \(E\), \(\tau(a)\) is an edit of \(E\). \(\operatorname{Ans}_S\) is defined on edits of \(S\). Unless \(E = S\) (the transport is to the simulation layer), \(\operatorname{Ans}_S(\tau(a), \sigma(b))\) does not type-check. The expectation is therefore defined only for transports whose target is \(S\), not for every transport.

The old preamble ("Let \(t\) be selected on history \(H\)") was implicitly about the selected \(P\)-to-\(S\) transport, where \(E = S\) and \(\operatorname{Ans}_S\) is well-defined. The new preamble removes this restriction without adjusting the definition. The declaration's "for every transport" is therefore false: violation (fidelity failure) is defined for every transport, but expectation is not.

**Repair:** Change the declaration to "Part IV now defines expectation and violation for every transport to the simulation layer" or, better, change the expectation definition from \(\operatorname{Ans}_S\) to \(\operatorname{Ans}_E\) where \(E\) is the target of the transport.

### 2. R07 — (a): the declaration misses a claim.

The new wording (lines 75-77) states three claims:
(i) "Which organizations a carrier can bear, and whether what carriers of one physical medium bear can pass to carriers of another, are fixed by the adopted physics."
(ii) "substrate independence holds so far as that physics lets contents pass between media."
(iii) "Where two media cannot exchange what they bear, the contents that only one of them can bear form, for a system built of the other, a barrier in the sense of Part XIII."

The declaration captures (ii) and (iii) but not (i). Claim (i) is stronger than the existing text's "Every attribution of an organization to a physical system must be permitted by the adopted physics" — "fixed by" asserts determinacy (the physics determines the full set of bearable organizations), while "permitted by" asserts only a constraint. The declaration is incomplete.

**Repair:** Add to the declaration: "and that which organizations a carrier can bear and whether contents pass between media are fixed by the adopted physics."

### 3. R30 — (c): the criteria for identifying a binding by its use diverge from Part IX's reason use while citing it as the model, and the declaration's "in the manner of reason use" is inaccurate.

The new text (lines 403-405) says a construction witness may identify a binding "by responses that preserve its role bindings, send content-preserving recodings to the same transition and content changes to the changes the binding specifies, and lie on an active route, as reason use asks of an objection (Part IX)."

Part IX's reason use says "sends content changes to the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule." The new text replaces "the operative deliberative rule" with "the binding." For a structural binding (e.g., "the binding of a persistence component to a continuity subnetwork," Derivation 10), "the changes the binding specifies" are structural changes — not the deliberative changes of Part IX's rule. The pointer "(Part IX)" therefore does not say what the change says it says: Part IX's definition uses a different source of specification.

The declaration's "in the manner of reason use" is correspondingly imprecise: the manner is modeled on reason use but differs in one criterion.

**Repair:** Either change "the changes the binding specifies" to "the changes specified by the operative deliberative rule" (to match Part IX), or change "as reason use asks of an objection (Part IX)" to "in a manner modeled on reason use (Part IX)" and adjust the declaration to "modeled on the manner of reason use."

### 4. R13 — (a): the declaration does not capture the first clause of the new wording.

R13's new wording (line 223) begins "Expectation and violation are defined for every transport, surprise only for a selected one." This clause is not in R13's declaration. It restates R12's change, so it is not a new claim. But the declaration is meant to say exactly what changes in what is claimed; the restatement is part of the new wording and is not mentioned. Minor.

### 5. R25 — (a): the declaration does not characterize "bare denial."

The new text (line 335) says "a bare denial, which offers no component that responds to a change in what produces the appearance, is not an account of it." The inline clause "which offers no component that responds to a change in what produces the appearance" defines "bare denial." The declaration says "a bare denial is not an account" without this characterization. The characterization is part of the claim (it defines the term), and the declaration should mention it or paraphrase it. Minor.
